B. Prosecutorial misconduct claims previously 
considered by this Court 


From among these instances of prosecutorial nonfeasance 
and misfeasance, referred to above, this Court has already 
considered on the merits the claims found at 4 D.1.(1) (the 
prosecution’s failure to produce the 1972 murder trial 
transcript); and § D.1.(7) (the prosecution’s failure to produce 
lab reports indicating no blood stains on clothes). 

With regard to ¥ D.1.(1) (the prosecution’s failure to 
produce the 1972 murder trial transcript), this Court evaluated 
the claim in its opinion, 999 F.Supp. at 1089-90, and 
concluded that the 1972 transcript “would not have affected 
the result” of the guilt stage, Jd. at 1089, and was “not 
persuaded” that its absence “undermine[d] confidence in the 
outcome of the sentencing hearing,”/d. at 1090. The Court 
specifically noted that it reached this conclusion considering 
the effect of the absence of the 1972 transcript “standing 
alone,” Jd. Petitioner asks this Court to reconsider that 
conclusion for the reasons set out in this memorandum upon 
consideration of the cumulative effect of other identified 
errors, and not “standing alone.” 

With regard to §D.1.1(7) (the prosecution’s failure to 
produce lab reports indicating no blood stains on clothes), this 
court concluded that it was not a proper Brady claim, because 
defense counsel could have had access to the information 
from sources other than the prosecutor. /d. at 1090. If it is 
not a proper Brady claim, by this Court’s own conclusion it is 
necessarily an instance of ineffective assistance of counsel. It 
is one or the other, See, Banks v Reynolds, 54 F.3d 1508, 
1515, n. 14 (10" Cir. 1995) (recognizing the propriety of 
considering claims, such as this, as alternatively either 
prosecutorial misconduct or ineffective assistance of counsel); 
and, Petitioner has, in fact, plead it in the alternative, See, 
Amended Petition, §§ D.(1)(7) (prosecutorial misconduct); 
and, A.(1) and (2); E.(2)(a) and (e) (ineffective assistance of 
counsel). This Memorandum, and attached appendices, 


248 


together with the reaction of jurors, as reflected by their 
Affidavits, filed separately, makes clear the prejudice suffered 
by Petitioner at guilt and at sentencing created by the absence 
of this evidence in the jury’s deliberation. 
Cc. Prosecutorial misconduct claims previously 
considered by the Tennessee Supreme 
Court. 


In addition to the instances of prosecutorial misconduct 
identified, above, on the direct appeal of this case from trial, 
the Tennessee Supreme Court considered on the merits the 
fraction of prosecutorial misconduct claims that were 
revealed on the face of the trial court record. With regard to 
these relatively few instances of prosecutorial misconduct in 
the record at the time, the Tennessee Supreme Court found 
that the “conduct of the State’s attorney [Zimmermann] 
bordered on deception” and “was improper,” but, nevertheless 
concluded that these instances of prosecutorial misconduct 
did not require reversal. State v Jones, 789 S.W.2d 545, 552 
(Tenn. 1990). The Tennessee Supreme Court did not 
consider these instances of prosecutorial misconduct 
cumulatively and collectively with other instances of 
prosecutorial misconduct, all instances of ineffective 
assistance of counsel, and the other errors committed in this 
case. This Court is in a position to consider these claims 
along with other errors reflected in the record that is now 
complete. 


D. Instances of ineffective assistance of counsel. 


At the trial of this case, Petitioner had viable defenses in 
the guilt stage that he was not the assailant and that, whether 
he was the assailant or not, he was insane at the time of the 
offense, both of which would have been consistent and 
complementary with the mitigation that he had to present at 
the sentencing stage of the trial. In spite of the availability of 
these defenses, defense counsel did not investigate potential 
defenses and did not present any defense, did not call any 
witnesses, and did not present any proof at all. At the 


249 


sentencing stage of the trial, Petitioner had an abundance of 
available mitigating evidence of which this Court is well 
familiar and which was addressed in this Court’s opinion. 
See 999 F.Supp. at 1096-1102. Notwithstanding the 
availability of this abundance of mitigating evidence, defense 
counsel did not investigate it and was unaware of it. 
Consequently, he did not present it to the jury. See, Appendix 
A. 

All of the state and federal courts sitting in collateral 
review of this conviction and sentence have agreed that the 
performance of counsel was constitutionally inadequate at 
both the guilt and sentencing stages. See, 999 F.Supp. at 
1095. The only question among the courts was that of 
whether sufficient prejudice was created by the failures of 
counsel to warrant reversal. Since the only question was the 
sufficiency of the prejudice caused by counsel’s recognized 
failures, then a review of claims becomes only a question of 
measuring the degree and impact of the prejudice. Whatever 
degree of prejudice exists at the guilt stage, as well as at the 
sentencing stage, whether or not it is sufficient standing alone 
to warrant reversal, must be considered collectively and 
cumulatively with other errors committed in the case in order 
to determine whether the cumulation of error warrants 
reversal. 

From among the reviewing federal judges, including this 
Court and the Circuit panel, two of the four judges, who have 
reviewed the case, have found the prejudice sufficient to set 
side the sentence of death. Yet, none of the federal judges, 
including the two who decided to set the sentence of death 
aside, considered any issue other than the single question of 
whether the sentence of death should be set aside due to 
counsel’s ineffectiveness at the sentencing stage. None of the 
judges considered any of the other claims properly raised by 
Petitioner, including the errors of counsel other than those 
committed in the sentencing stage of the trial, including the 
myriad instances of prosecutorial misconduct, and including 
any other errors committed in this case, cumulatively and 


250 


collectively with the claim of ineffective assistance of counsel 
at the sentencing stage of the trial. 


Pursuant to T.C.A. § 39-2-203(h), a Tennessee capital 
defendant will avoid a sentence of death, if only a single juror 
holds out for a life sentence. Consequently, the prejudice 
determination involves only a determination of whether 
confidence in the ou.come is sufficiently undermined so that 
“one juror could have found that he did not deserve the death 
penalty.” (Emphasis added.) Carter v Bell, 218 F.3d 581, 
592 (6" Cir. 2000) (single juror prejudice rule applied in 
Tennessee case); see, also, Coleman v Mitchell, 2001 WL 
1194919, Sixth Circuit No. 98-3545, filed October 10, 2001 
at 28 (single juror prejudice rule applied in a Ohio case). This 
standard has easily been met upon a collective evaluation of 
the universe of error that occurred in this trial. See, Jurors 
Affidavits, filed in separate pleading. 

Tennessee law specifically recognizes lingering or 
residual doubt about guilt as proper mitigating evidence. See, 
State v Teague, 897 S.W.2d 248 (Tenn. 1995). In fact, 
Tennessee law requires the jury to consider the proof 
presented in the guilt stage during its deliberation in the 
sentencing stage. See T. C. A. § 39-2-203(e) (jury is directed 
by the instructing court to consider “any mitigating 
circumstances raised by the evidence at either the guilt or 
sentencing hearing or both”). If no defenses are presented 
during the guilt stage of the trial, as occurred in this case, 
nothing is presented in that stage for the jurors to consider in 
the sentencing stage, though viable defenses may have 
existed, as they did in this case. 

The available and viable guilt stage defenses that trial 
counsel failed to present would have been persuasive 
mitigating evidence. Indeed, the available but unpresented 
guilt-stage defenses (i.e. that Petitioner was not the assailant, 
and that Petitioner did not have sufficient mens rea) may have 
been the strongest available mitigating evidence that 
Petitioner could have presented in this case. Assistant District 


251 


Attorney John Zimmermann testified that he anticipated it 
would be difficult to persuade the jurors to impose a sentence 
of death in this case. He stated that, in order to obtain a death 
verdict, it would be necessary to convince the jurors that 
Petitioner was the assailant “not beyond a reasonable doubt, 
but beyond a shadow of a doubt,” FT 905. See, generally, FT 
905-908. His opinion in that regard is reflected to be true in 
other cases, as well. In a study entitled Why Jurors Vote Life 
or Death: Operative Facts in Ten Florida Death Penalty 
Cases, Geimer and Amsterdam, 15 Am. J. Crim. Law 1 
(1988), the authors concluded that the existence of some 
degree of doubt about the guilt of the accused was 
overwhelmingly the most often recurring factor in the juror’s 
vote for a life sentence. See, particularly, 15 Am. J. Crim. 
Law at 28-34. In that study, 69% of the jurors interviewed 
identified “lingering” doubt about guilt as the “operative 
factor” in the vote for life. See, Table 2, 15 Am. J. Crim. Law 
at 27. Though'guilt stage defenses may be considered to be 
the strongest mitigating evidence, in the determination of 
whether counsel was ineffective in this case, his failure to 
present guilt stage defenses has never been considered 
collectively with other errors in the determination of whether 
Petitioner’s sentence of death should be set aside. 


In order to properly evaluate the fairness and the 
reliability of this trial, it is incumbent on this Court to 
consider the total failures of defense counsel, both his failure 
to affirmatively present any of Petitioner’s available defenses 
at both stages of the trial as well as his failure to rebut the 
fraudulent case presented by the prosecution, collectively and 
cumulatively with the instances of prosecutorial withholding 
of exculpatory evidence and the prosecutor’s intentional 
deceit of Petitioner’s evaluators, his defense counsel, the 
court, and the jury. Upon that evaluation, it is obvious that it 
would be a travesty of justice to allow this conviction and 
sentence to stand. 


252 


VI. Conclusion 

For the foregoing reasons, this Court should grant 
Petitioner’s Rule 60 motion and reverse its earlier dismissal 
of the substantial body of Petitioner’s prosecutorial 
misconduct claims. 


This Court should consider the merits of Petitioner’s 
prosecutorial misconduct claims based upon the evidentiary 
record already created in this case. 

In addressing Petitioner’s prosecutorial misconduct 
claims, this Court should consider the claims both 
individually and cumulatively with all of Petitioner’s other 
claims, including the claims of ineffective assistance of 
counsel at both the guilt and sentencing stages of Petitioner’s 
case. The proof in this habeas case establishes prosecutorial 
fraud and deceit which constitute per se violations of 
Petitioner’s constitutional rights requiring reversal of 
Petitioner’s convictions and his death sentence. Viewed 
cumulatively with the other errors in Petitioner’s case, 
including but not limited to the errors arising from the 
ineffectiveness of Petitioner’s trial counsel, the proof in this 
habeas case establishes that Petitioner was grossly prejudiced 
at both the guilt and sentencing stages of his case. 

Accordingly, this Court, upon considering all of 
Petitioner’s claims, must grant Petitioner a writ of habeas 
corpus setting aside Petitioner’s convictions and death 
sentence.

(ii) | Zimmermann’s pattern of “misleading” 
defense counsel and the court; and 

(iii) | Zimmernrann’s pattern of knowingly violating 
court rulings relating to inadmissibility of evidence and 
improper jury argument. 

Some of these court opinions are listed below: 

1. In the direct appeal of Abu-Ali’s case from the

reprimanded by the Tennessee Supreme Court for handing 
inadmissible material to the jury during Abu-Ali’s sentencing 
hearing. The Tennessee Supreme Court said: 

Defendant made a motion in limine prior to the 

sentencing hearing to prohibit any mention at the 

hearing that defendant was on parole at the time of 

the offense. In the course of arguing the motion the 

State’s attorney stated he would not pass the records 

of defendant’s prior murder conviction to the jury 

since they showed that defendant had received a life 

sentence and described the circumstances of the 

crime. .... The State’s attorney then passed to the 

jury the record of defendant’s conviction for assault 

with a deadly weapon which included a two-count 

indictment charging him with robbery in addition to 

the assault. Defense counsel raised an objection on 


255 


the basis that the information it contained was 
irrelevant and requested the court to instruct the jury 
that the only information contained in the record 
which was applicable was the fact of the conviction. 
The trial judge gave the instruction requested. 

The defense objection was appropriate. The 
conduct of the State’s attorney bordered on 
deception by which he was able to get before the 
jury information which was not evidence ii the case 
they had under consideration. The action of the 
State was improper. 

State v. Jones, 789 S.W.2d 545, 551-2 (Tenn. 1990). The 
Tennessee Supreme Court held, however, that this 
prosecutorial misconduct was “harmless,” and therefore 
denied this claim as a basis for reversing the death sentence. 

2. In the federal district court habeas proceeding, 

Judge Campbell held that Zimmermann committed a Brady 
violation by failing to turn over to Abu-Ali’s trial counsel the 
transcript of Abu-Ali’s prior (1972) murder trial. This 
transcript contained testimony of two psychiatrists, one for 
the defense and the other for the prosecution, which described 
aspects of Abu-Ali’s mental illnesses and would have 
supported an insanity defense under Tennessee’s definition of 
insanity. Although Judge Campbell found that Zimmermarin 
had violated his Brady obligation to turn over this exculpatory 
evidence, Judge Campbell also found that, “standing alone,” 
this instance of prosecutorial misconduct did not 
“undermine[s] confidence in the outcome of the sentencing 
hearing,” and therefore he rejected this claim as a basis for 
awarding Abu-Ali relief. Abu-Ali Abdur Rahman v. Bell, 999 
F.Supp. 1073, 1089-90 (M.D.Tenn. 1998). Judge Campbell, 
however, did grant relief on grounds of ineffective assistance 
of counsel. 

+ In In re Zimmerman, 1986 WL 8586 

(Tenn.Crim.App. 1986), Zimmerman was held in contempt of 
court for violating Rule 16, Tenn.R.Crim.P., which is the rule 


256 


requiring the prosecution to disclose certain types of evidence 
to the defense prior to trial. Zimmermann argued that his 
noncompliance with Rule 16 “was clearly made through 
oversight” (his words) and was not “willful.” The Court of 
Criminal Appeals said that this was not an excuse and 
characterized Zimmermann’s actions as an “abuse of, or 
unlawful interference with, the process or proceedings of 
the court.” 
5. In Zimmermann v. Board of Professional 
Responsibility, 764 S.W.2¢ 757 (1989), Zimmermann was 
sanctioned for making inappropriate comments to the press in 
violation of the disciplinary rules. The court rejected his 
argument that his comments were protected by the First 
Amendment. 
6. According to a Tennessean article published 
May 30, 1998, in the case of State v. Vukelich Zimmermann 
was chastised by Davidson County Circuit Court Judge 
Gayden for “misleading” the court into improperly issuing a 
seizure order for the seizure of a $102,000 investment account 
owned by the defendant and his wife which allegedly 
contained the proceeds of drug sales. According to the 
article: 
Circuit Judge Hamilton Gayden testified that he 
should not have signed the original seizure order 
March 13. Gayden said that, “upon reflection,” he 
believes Zimmermann and Metro Drug investigator 
Ed Rigsby did not present enough evidence about 
the source of the funds to justify seizing them as the 
fruits of illegal drug dealing... The judge testified 
that Zimmermann and Rigsby misled him into 
believing that the money in the mutual fund account 
came from illegal activity.” 

Judge Gayden set aside the seizure order and directed that the 

investment account be returned to the Vukelichs. 

7. In State v. Middlebrooks, 995 S.W.2d 550 
(1999), a death penalty case, Zimmermann was reprimanded 


257 


for making two kinds of improper closing arguments to the 
jury at the conclusion of capital sentencing hearing. First, the 
court found that Zimmermann’s “victim impact” argument 
was improper: 
[T}he prosecutor’s statement, “his family asks you 
to impose the death penalty,” was improper. 
Although evidence to this effect was not introduced 
during the sentencing hearing, and would have been 
inadmissible, the prosecutor’s statement clearly is 
an improper characterization of the family’s view as 
to the appropriate sentence. 
Id. at 558. Secondly, the court found that Zimmermann’s 
biblical references during closing argument (which were 
extensive) were also improper: 
We have condemned Biblical and scriptural 
references in a prosecutor’s closing argument so 
frequently that it is difficult not to conclude that the 
remarks in this case were made either with blatant 
disregard for our decisions or a level of 
astonishing ignorance of the state of the law in 
this regard.... As we said in Cribbs, “in the face of 
this clear and longstanding precedent, the repeated 
introduction by prosecutors of such references 
into trials of serious criminal offenses is 
inexplicabie.” 
Id. at 559. Although the court found these arguments to be 
improper, it also determined that these errors were harmless 
and therefore the court did not reverse the judgment below. 


8. The more recent case of Garrett v. State, 2001 
Tenn.Cnm.App. LEXIS 206 (2001), presents a particularly 
egregious example of prosecutorial misconduct. The 
defendant was convicted of first degree murder and sentenced 
to life. The prosecution’s theory at trial was that the 
defendant locked the victim in the room of a house and then 
burned the house down, killing the victim. A key factual 
issue at trial was whether the room where the victim was 


— 


258 


found had been locked from the outside, which is what the 
prosecution alleged. Zimmermann had in his files an eleven- 
page report by an investigator which described a conversation 
with the Fire Detective who told the author of the report that 
the door to the room was, in fact, unlocked. Zimmerman 
never turned his report over to the defense counsel: In the 
post-conviction hearing, Zimmermann explained that he did 
not turn over this report to the defense because , essentially, 
he felt this information was unreliable. The Tennessee Court 
of Appeals rejected Zimmermann’s explanation and found 
that he committed a Brady violation. Consequently, the court 
set aside the conviction and granted the defendant a new trial. 
The Court said: . 
We are most troubled with the eleven-page Miller 
Report, which was not disclosed to the defendant... 
The State contends that this report was neither 
withheld, exculpatory, nor material. We disagree in 
all respects. The evidence in the post-conviction 
hearing showed that the prosecutor led defense 
counsel to believe that the State had no information 
-about the locked or unlocked status of the utility 
room door. However, at trial the State’s witnesses 
testified that the utility room door was locked and 
the prosecutor submitted to the jury as the theory of 
the State’s case that the defendant locked the victim 
in the utility room and set the house on fire. 
Therefore, the State did have, prior to trial, 
information that the door was locked. In fact, not 
only did the State have information that the door to 


259 


counsel that the door was unlocked then this 
information was not withheld. The State’s assertion 
that the defendant was aware that the State had no 
information that the door was locked is undermined 
by the State relying so heavily at trial on testimony 
that the door was locked. Assistant District 
Attorney General Zimmerman, when questioned at 
the post conviction hearing as to why he did not 
include the eleven-page report in discover, stated, “I 
felt like Detective Miller’s recollection was vague, 
he could not specifically remember the conversation 
he had with Captain Jenkins, and all he knew is 
what he had recorded in his report, which was 
equivocal, at best, and Jenkins was absolutely clear 
on it.” This Court is extremely troubled with 
General Zimmerman’s decision to himself 
determine the reliability of the evidence and to 
refuse to turn over evidence he believes is 
unreliable, especially when the evidence is 
requested and is exculpatory. 

Essentially, the State intentionally withheld the 
information by leading the defendant to believe 
they had no information about the status of the door 
and then pursuing the very opposite theory at trial: 
that the defendant had locked the victim in the 
utility room and set the house on fire. We agree 
with the defendant that the information was 
withheld.

LEXIS 734 (Sept. 11, 2001), was a drug trafficking and 
money laundering case that went to trial two times. In the 
first trial, Zimmermann asked a witness whether he had 
smoked marijuana with the defendant on two prior occasions. 
The trial judge ruled those questions to be irrelevant and 
inadmissible. In the second trial, despite the trial judge’s 
ruling in the first trial, Zimmermann asked the same witness 
the very same questions. As described by the appeliate court: 


260 


The [trial] court immediately asked for a bench 
conference and excused the jury. The prosecutor 
apologized and claimed to have forgotten the trial 
court’s earlier ruling, and the trial court strongly 
admonished the prosecutor. 
Later in the opinion, the appellate court said that “the 
testimony solicited was patently improper given the tral 
court’s previous ruling.” The appellate court went on to say, 
however, that this error was harmless because the other

ee Se eee 


261 


EVIDENCE THAT PETITIONER WAS NOT 


THE ASSAILANT, Appendix E To Petitioner’s 
Memorandum In Support Of Motion For Relief From 
Judgment Pursuant To Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(B). 


The evidence in this case demonstrates that Petitioner 
was not the assailant who killed the murder victim, Patrick 
Daniels. At the very least, the evidence raises reasonable 
doubt about whether Petitioner was the assailant. 
Accordingly, the evidence fails to establish that Petitioner is 
guilty of premeditated murder. For this reason alone, 
Petitioner should be entitled tc a new trial regarding guilt. 
Moreover, the blood evidence clearly creates residual doubt 
about whether Petitioner was the assailant, which is an 
additional mitigating factor that must considered in this case. 
State v. Teague, 897 S.W.2d 248, 256 (Tenn. 1995); State v. 
Hartman, 42 S.W.3d 44 (Tenn. 2001). The effect of the 
unpresented blood evidence must be cumulated with the 
prejudicial effects of the other errors in this case. 


A. According to the blood evidence, the assailant 
would have had blood stains on his clothes. 


In the face of the crime scene photos and the police 
reports describing the extensive blood splattering, even 
Zimmermann testified before this Court that the assailant 
almost certainly would have had blood on his clothes. (FEx. 
2-3; FT. 917). Dr. Kris Sperry gave his unrebutted expert 
testimony that, given the nature of the victim’s wounds and 
the amount of blood splattering, the assailant certainly would 
have had blood on his clothes. (FT. 37-8, 77). 


B. Petitioner’s clothes tested negative for blood stains. 


Petitioner’s clothes tested negative for blood stains. 
These clothes included Petitioner’s long dark wool coat that 
he was wearing on the night of the offense. (FEx. 115, 117). 
According to Dr. Sperry, the tests performed by the T.B.I. 
crime lab would have detected the blood stains. (FT. 38, 61). 
He also testified that the blood stains could not have been 
cleansed from the wool fabric of the coat without 


262 


permanently damaging the fabric. (FT. 59-61). 


& Therefore, because Petitioner was wearing his long 
black wool coat at the time, he could not have been 


the assailant. 


The prosecution recognized that the absence of blood on 
Petitioner’s clothes was a weakness in their case. See 
Zimmermann’s internal memo (FEx. “2, at Bates stamp p. 
000678). As acknowledged by this Court, the only way to 
reconcile the blood evidence with the charge that Petitioner 
was the assailant would be to assume that Petitioner somehow 
was not wearing the coat at the time of the assault. (See this 
Court’s opinion, 999 F.Supp. 1085). There are at least two 
problems with this assumption: 

First, all of the evidence in the case tends to show that 
Petitioner wore his long dark wool coat at the time of the 
offense. Norma Norman tesiified that Petitioner was wearing 
the coat from the time he entered the apartment on the night 
of the offense to the time she was tied up and blindfolded. (T. 
1332, 1334, 1343, 1364, 1366-7). Devalle Miller, the co- 
defendant, also testified that Petitioner was wearing the long 
wool coat at the time of the offense. (T. 1451, 1454, 1462, 
1465). The prosecution introduced this same wool coat into 
evidence (as Trial Exhibit 12), identified the coat as the one 
that Petitioner was wearing at the time, and showed the coat 
to the jury during the guilt stage closing argument in support 
of their argument that Petitioner was the assailant. (T. 1333, 
1672-3). 

Second, as described in Petitioner’s briefing to this Court, 
the evidence in this habeas proceeding establishes that the 
prosecution spent literally hours preparing Miller, the only 
eyewitness to the assault, to testify at Petitioner’s trial. At no 
time has Miller ever said that Petitioner took off his coat 
during the assault. Given the prosecution’s acknowledgement 
of the weakness in their case raised by the blood evidence, if 
Petitioner did take his coat off forthe assault, then certainly 
the prosecution would have elicited that information from 


a oC 


263 


Miller. Because Miller never said that Petitioner was without 
his coat, and because there is absolutely no other evidence 
that even hints that Petitioner took off his coat at the time of 
the assault, the inescapable conclusion is that Petitioner wore 
the coat throughout the offense including the precise time 
when the assault occurred. 


D. The evidence supports the proposition that Miller 
was the assailant. 


By contrast, there are a number of reasons to suspect that 
Miller in fact was the assailant. These reasons include the 
following: 


1. According to Norman’s testimony; while Petitioner 
and Miller were in the apartment, Miller was standing in the 
kitchen area. Accordingly, he would have had easy access to 
the knife that was used in the assault. (T. 1344). 


2. Also according to Norman’s testimony, before she 
was tied up, Miller was in a panic and threatened to kill the 
dogs. (T. 1371). He therefore was obviously fearful of how 
the dogs might draw attention to what they were doing, and it 
can be assumed that he would have been fearful of leaving 
either Daniels or Norman as witnesses. If in his panic he 
threatened to kill the dogs, it is reasonable to imagine that he 
would have been willing to kill Daniels and Norman. 

3. On the day of Petitioner’s arrest, two days after the 
offense, when Petitioner’s wife was driving Miller to his 
apartment, Miller acted strangely by taking off his field jacket 
and work shirt and throwing them in the back of the car. His 
trial testimony on this went as follows: 

Q. Did you leave anything in the car that day? 

A. Yes, I did. I was pretty scared and pretty 

uptight. I took off my - I had a field jacket on and a 

work shirt, and I left them in the car. 

Q. Where did you place that field jacket and work 

shirt? 

A. I just threw them in the back. 


264 


Q. Mr. Miller, can you explain what you did 
that for? 

A. I was just panicking and scared. 

Q. Panicking and scared about what? 


A. I feared - I thought that, you know, 

somebody might recognize me walking back to 

my apartment, and, you know, I just thought I 

might get picked up, so I took - threw the jacket 

and the shirt off. 
(T. 1481). Miller’s explanation for this unusual behavior was 
not satisfactory. This behavior reveals a fearful response to 
the situation, which would be consistent with the idea that he 
was the assailant. The most logical explanation of this 
behavior is that Miller did not want to be caught wearing 
clothes that might contain signs of the assault, i.e. blood 
stains. It is significant that Miller’s clothes never were 
tested for blood stains. 


4. Most significantly, in his original statement to the 
prosecution and at Petitioner’s trial, Miller gave clearly 
fabricated descriptions of the assault. In his original statement 
to the prosecution, Miller said Petitioner “proceeded to stab 
[Daniels] numerous times uh both in the back and in the 
chest.” (FEx. 51, at Bates stamp p. 000166). At Petitioner’s 
trial, Miller testified that immediately after the assault, 
Daniels “was laying on the floor. And he wes - he was face 
down, I believe - he was face down. And he was just going 
into convulsions, ...” (T. 1472; See, also, FEx. 119). Dr. 
Sperry testified, however, that Miller’s testimony contradicted 
the physical evidence. (FT. 47-50). Dr. Sperry pointed out 
that the medical examiner’s autopsy report indicated that 
Daniels suffered stab wounds only in the front chest area, and 
not in his back as Miller had testified. (See, also, the autopsy 
report and Dr. Harlan’s trial testimony: FEx. 14, 113 and 114; 
T. 1658). Dr. Sperry further testified that, based on the blood 
patterns revealed by the crime scene photos, Daniels could 
not have been lying face down, again contrary to Miller’s 


265 


testimony. (See, also, FEx. 3, the police report describing 
Daniels as “laying face up on the floor.”) Again, Dr. Sperry’s 
testimony on these points was unrebutted, and the state has 
offered no explanation to reconcile Miller’s testimony with 
the physical evidence. 

The fact that Miller gave fabricated descriptions of what 
actually happened with Daniels demonstrates that he was 
making things up. Given that he confessed to participating in 
the events leading up to the assault and was prepared to plead 
guilty to second degree murder in exchange for his testimony, 
there is only one plausible reason for Miller’s fabricated 
account of the assault: there was something important about 
the assault that he wanted to hide. The best explanation is that 
Miller, in fact, was the assailant. 


Other than Miller’s own testimony, which is inherently 
unreliable, all of the evidence in the case is consistent with 
the theory that Miller was the assailant. 


D. Petitioner’s sentencing hearing “confession” does 
not rebut these conclusions. 


In light of the foregoing evidence, the state has offered 
only one rebuttal to the theory that Petitioner was not the 
assailant. The state relies on Petitioner’s sentencing hearing 
testimony where the state alleges that he confessed to the 
killing. This rebuttal, however, is flawed for the following 
reasons: 


1. Every witness who has spent time with Petitioner 
during the period after the offense has testified that Petitioner 
has consistently reported that he does not remember what 
happened when Daniels was killed. See testimony of Susie 
Bynum (FT. 1245), Neal McAlpin (FT. 212), Sumter Camp 
(FT. 732-3), Richard Dinkins (FT. 888), and Dr. Sadoff (FT. 
471-2, 485). The MTMHI records also reflect that during 
their evaluation of him, he lacked any recollection of what 
happened at the time of the assault. In the Interdisciplinary 
Notes dated 2/11/87 from the MTMHI file, Dr. Marshall 
reported, “When asked about killing the allegedly [sic] 


266 


victims he said I don’t know if I killed him or not.” (FEx. 
125). 

2. Contrary to the state’s assertions, Petitioner’s 
sentencing hearing “confession” did not reflect a recollection 
of what actually happened. Nowhere in his sentencing hearing 
testimony did Petitioner ever say that he recalled stabbing 
either of the victims; and in fact he tried to say that he did not 
have any such recollection. (See, e.g., T. 1865, where 
Petitioner said, “I don’t remember,” and T. 1996, where 
Petitioner said that he was admitting to the killings because of 
“information you [the prosecutor] have given,” and not 
because of his own recollection of what happened). The exact 
nature of Petitioner’s testimony, and the fact that he never 
admitted to having a specific recollection of the assault, was 
discussed in some detail during Dr. Sadoff’s testimony before 
this Court. (FT. 486-497). 

3. The fact that Petitioner has no recollection of what 
happened is consistent with his documented history and his 
mental illness. As Dr. Sadoff testified, and as reflected in 
Petitioner’s social history, Petitioner has a history of 
“hysterical blindness” or blackouts. Also as Dr. Sadoff 
explained, this kind of history is characteristic of someone 
like Petitioner who suffers from Post-Traumatic Stress 
Disorder and Borderline Personality Disorder. (FT. 463, 472- 
3). 

4. To the extent Petitioner may have made a confession 
during his sentencing hearing testimony, that confession was 
not reliable. Due to his mental illnesses, Petitioner is 
emotionally unstable. (FT. 467, 483). Petitioner received 
ineffective representation at trial and he therefore presumably 

felt unprotected when he was called to the witness stand. He 
was never prepared for his testimony, and in fact did not 
know he was about to testify until ~oments before he was 
called to the stand. (FT. 731-2, 765). His “confession” 
occurred under relentless cross examination, and he basically 
broke down on the stand such that the trial court found it 


267 


necessary to call a recess so that he could compose himself. 
(T. 1871, FT. 731). Sumter Camp testified before this Court 
that because of Petitioner’s emotional instability and lack of 
preparation, Petitioner was not in a condition to testify, and 
his testimony was “one of the saddest things I have seen in 
my legal career.” (FT. 1731). Moreover, Petitioner had just 
been found guilty of first degree murder by the same jury that 
was then considering whether to impose the death penalty, 
and his only option was to plead for mercy. (See Lionel 
Barrett’s testimony on this point where he said that his only 
strategy at sentencing was based on a “hope” for “mercy.” 
FT.378-9). Petitioner had heard Miller testify that Petitioner 
was the assailant. Also, Petitioner had every reason to believe 
that, contrary to the truth, blood was found on his clothes. We 
know, for example, that when Petitioner was at MTMHI, at 
least, he was incorrectly told that blood was found on his 
pants. (FEx. 151, pp. 2-3). Petitioner’s trial counsel failed to 
learn of the blood evidence, and so Petitioner’s mistaken 

impression was never corrected. Petitioner, therefore, had no 

means of disputing the prosecution’s charge that he was the 

assailant, either to the court or in his own mind. 

Consequently, the most logical thing for him to do under all 

of these circumstances would be to admit to the killing in an 

effort to persuade the jury to spare his life. Under these 

circumstances, any “confession” that Petitioner may have 

given cannot be regarded as any kind of reliable account of 

what actually happened. Cf Herman v. Claudy, 350 U.S. 116, 

76 S.Ct. 223, 100 L.Ed. 126 (1956) (courtroom confession by 
defendant while pleading for mercy is not reliable and 
therefore not dispositive of defendant’s habeas claim).

I. 

JURISDICTION 
Although Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(a) specifically addresses the 

issue of a district court’s jurisdiction over an action while on 
appeal, Rule 60 (b) is silent in this regard. Lewis vy. 
Alexander, 987 F.2d 392, 394 (6th Cir. 1993). As a general 
rule, filing a notice of appeal divests the district court of 
jurisdiction and transfers jurisdiction to the appellate court. 
The district court retains jurisdiction only over matters that 
are in aid of appeal.* Jd. (citing Cochran v. Birkel, 651 F.2d 
1219, 1221 (6th Cir. 1981)). If the district court is disposed to 
grant a Rule 60 (b) motion filed there, the court may enter an 
order so certifying, and the moving party may then file a 
motion in the appellate court to remand the case. First Nat'l 
Bank Salem, Ohio v. Hirsch, 535 F.2d 343, 345-46 (6th Cir. 
1976) (per curiam). 

Il 


PROPRIETY OF A RULE 60(b) MOTION IN A 28 
U.S.C. § 2254 PROCEEDING 
Petitioner cites to Rodriguez v. Mitchell, 252 F.3d 
191, 199 (2d Cir. 2001), as authority for this Court to 


by this Court as no longer procedurally defaulted in reconsidering its 
judgment Respondent submits that such argument is appropriate only 
when and if this Court agrees that it may, and is so disposed, to entertain 
the Rule 60(b) motion. Therefore, respondent requests that he be afforded 
an appropriate period of time to respond to these procedurally defaulted 
claims if the Court should decide to entertain petitioner's Rule 60(b) 
motion. 

* Respondent notes at the outset that the Sixth Circuit has granted a 
Certificate of Appealability in another case on the following issue: 
"[W)hether, in light of Tennessee Supreme Court Rule 39, as amended 
June 28, 2001, Adams procedurally defaulted claim (7) . . .." Adams v. 
Holland, No. 00-6575 (6th Cir. Aug. 16, 2001) Certificate of 
Appealability granted (copy attached). 

* Because the mandate has not issued from the Sixth Circuit, it is 
respondent's position that this case is still on appeal. 


270 


entertain a Rule 60(b) motion in a federal habeas proceeding 
brought by a state prisoner. However, the Sixth Circuit has 
stated that “[w]e agree with those Circuits that have held that 
a Rule 60(b) motion is the practical equivalent of a successive 
habeas corpus petition. . . .” McQueen v. Scroggy, 99 F.3d 
1302, 1335 (6th Cir. 1996), reh'g and suggestion for reh’g 
en banc denied, cert. denied sub. nom., McQueen v. 
Parker, 520 U.S. 1257, 117 S. Ct. 2422, 138 L.Ed.2d 185 
(1997); accord United States v. Rich, 141 F.3d 550, 551 
(Sth Cir. 1998), cert. denied, 526 U.S. 1011, 119 S. Ct. 
1156, 143 L.Ed.2d 221 (1999); Thompson v. Calderon, 151 
F.3d 918, 921 (9th Cir. 1998) (en banc); Felker v. Turpin, 
101 F.3d 657, 660-61 (11th Cir. 1996) (per curiam); United 
States v. Hernandez, 158 F.Supp.2d 388, 391 (D.Del. 
2001).° 

Even if this Court were to find that the terms of the 
AEDPA’s limitations on second or successive applications, 
28 U.S.C. § 2244(b), do not govern this case, it “must be 
guided by the general principles underlying [the Supreme 
Court’s] habeas corpus jurisprudence.” Calderon vv. 
Thompson, 523 U.S. 538, 554, 118 S. Ct. 1489, 1500, 140 
L.Ed.2d 728 (1998). Because of “‘the profound societal 
costs’” attendant with the exercise of habeas jurisdiction, the 
Court has imposed significant limitations on the federal 
courts’ ability to grant habeas relief. Jd. at 555. Those 
limitations are based on the Court’s “enduring respect for ‘the 
State’s interest in the finality of convictions that have 
survived direct review within the state court system.”” Jd. 
When lengthy federal proceedings have run their course and a 
mandate denying relief should have, by all nghts, issued, see 
Fed. R. App. P. 41(d)(2)(D), finality acquires an added moral 


> McQueen went on to state that such a motion would be subject to a 
cause and prejudice analysis, which was the old standard under the abuse 
of the writ doctrine. Second and successive habeas petitions must now 
meet the criteria of 28 U.S.C. § 2244 (b) and be decided upon by the 
Circuit Court of Appeals. 


271 


dimension, because “[o]nly with an assurance of real finality 
can the State execute its moral judgment,” allowing victims to 
move forward with their lives. Jd. at 556. “To unsettle these 
expectations is to inflict a profound injury to the ‘powerful 
and legitimate interest in punishing the guilty.” Jd. 

Therefore, the proper procedure would be to transfer the 
matter to the Sixth Circuit for their determination of whether 
Geen Rule 60(b) motion satisfies the gateway criteria of 


Il. 


TENN. S. CT. R. 39 DOES NOT HAVE 
RETROSPECTIVE APPLICATION 


Tennessee Supreme Rule 39 reads as follows: 


In 1967, the General Assembly created the 
Tennessee Court of Criminal Appeals in order to 
reduce the appellate backlog in criminal cases. In 
most criminal and post-conviction cases, review of 
a final order of the Court of Criminal Appeals is not 
a matter of right, but of sound judicial discretion. 
Permission to appeal will be granted by this Court 
only where special and important reasons justify the 
exercise of that discretionary review power. Tenn. 
R. App. Proc. 11. We recognize that criminal and 
post-conviction relief litigants have routinely 
petitioned this Court for permission to appeal upon 
the Court of Criminal Appeals’ denial of relief in 
order to exhaust all available state remedies for 
purposes of federal habeas corpus litigation. In 
order to clarify that denial of relief by Court of 
Criminal Appeals shall constitute exhaustion of 
State remedies for federal habeas corpus purposes, 
we hereby adopt the following Rule 39, Rules of the 
Supreme Court, as stated below. 

In all appeals from criminal convictions or 

post-conviction relief matters from and after 

July 1, 1967, a litigant shall not be required to 


272 


petition for a rehearing or to file an 
application for permission to appeal to the 
Supreme Court of Tennessee following an 
adverse decision of the Court of Criminal 
Appeals in order to be deemed to have 
exhausted all available state remedies 
respecting a claim of error. Rather, when the 
claim has been presented to the Court of 
Criminal Appeals or the Supreme Court, and 
relief has been denied, the litigant shall be 
deemed to have exhausted all available state 
remedies available for that claim. On 
automatic review of capital cases by the 
Supreme Court pursuant to Tennessee Code 
Annotated, § 39-13-206, a claim presented to 
the Court of Criminal Appeals shall be 
considered exhausted even when such claim is 
not renewed in the Supreme Court on 
automatic review. 

“Statutes, regulations, and rules of court must be read in a 
“straightforward” and “commonsense” manner.” /n re 
Laurain, 113 F.3d 595, 597 (6™ Cir. 1997); of. Duncan v. 
Walker, 531 U.S. 991, _, 121 S. Ct. 2120, 2124-25, 150 
L.Ed.2d 251 (2001) (statutory construction: begins with 
language of statute, with duty to give effect, if possible, to 
every clause and word); Seals v. State, 23 S.W.3d 272, 276 
(Tenn. 2000) (basic principle of statutory construction Is to 
ascertain and give effect to legislative intent, without unduly 
restricting or expanding intended scope). 

Petitioner relies on Rule 39 for the proposition that it 
applies retrospectively to remove discretionary appeal to the 
Tennessee Supreme Court from the state review process. 
Respondent submits, however, that Rule 39 does not apply 
retrospectively. 

First, if the Tennessee Supreme Court intended for Rule 
39 to have retrospective application it could have clearly and 


273 


unambiguously said so. It did not. 


Second, a careful reading of Rule 39 shows that the 
operative word is “shall,” a word clearly and unambiguously 
signaling future conduct, e.g., “a litigant shall not be required 
to petition for a rehearing or to file an application for 
permission to appeal. . .,” and, “the litigant shall be deemed 
to have exhausted all available state remedies. . .” The only 
significance of the July 1, 1967 date mentioned is that this is 
the date that the Court of Criminal Appeals was created. 


Third, it is helpful to look at the standards applied by the 
Tennessee courts in order to determine whether or not a new 
rule, statute or other rule of law will be given retrospective 
application. | 

With regard to statutes, they do not operate 
retrospectively unless it is so provided in the statute. 
“However, statutes dealing with matters of procedure or 
remedy are generally held to be applicable to proceedings 
after enactment even though the facts occurred prior to 
enactment. Mangrum v. Wal-Mart Stores, Inc., 950 
S.W.2d 33, 37 (Tenn. App. 1997). Where there is a new law 
changing a rule of practice, the new rule is applicable to all 
cases then pending in the state system. Frame v. Marlin 
Firearms Company, Inc., 514 S.W.2d 728, 730 (Tenn. 
1974). 


Even when imprisonment or a death sentence are at issue, 
most changes in the law, including those of constitutional 
dimension, do not affect judgments that became final before 
the change occurred. See, e.g., Teague v. Lane, 489 U.S. 
288, 109 S.Ct. 1060, 103 L.Ed.2d 334 (1989). Petitioner’s 
case has been final so far as the state system is concerned 
since 10 June 1991. 


Furthermore, as discussed infra, the apparent benefits 
sought through enactment of Rule 39 cannot be achieved 
through retrospective application, rendering such application 
meaningless, because individuals such as petitioner cannot 
return to state court. 


274 


IV 


TENN. S. CT. R. 39 MAY NOT EXPRESSLY 
DIRECT FEDERAL COURTS TO DEEM CLAIMS 
EXHAUSTED 


Even if this Court disagrees with respondent and finds 
that the Tennessee Supreme Court meant for Tenn. S. Ct. R. 
39 to apply retrospectively, that intention is not binding on 
the federal courts. It is the federal courts, not the state courts, 
that must determine whether “available” remedies have been 
“exhausted” in the state courts before the federal courts can 
hear a claim. As such, the Supremacy Clause prevents Rule 
39 from being so directive to the federal courts. Mattis v. 
Vaughn, 128 F.Supp.2d 249, 259 (E.D. Pa. 2001).° 


To interpret Rule 39 as petitioner does would have the 
effect of saying that, even though Sixth Circuit and Supreme 
Court precedent make seeking discretionary review 
mandatory for exhaustion in the absence of such an order or 
rule, see O'Sullivan v. Boerckel, 526 U.S. 838, 842, 119 
S.Ct. 1728, 1731, 144 L.Ed.2d 1 (1999); Manning v. 
Alexander, 912 F.2d 878, 881 (6th Cir. 1990), this Court is 
now required to treat such review as not having been 
available in the past. This too, would be a Supremacy Clause 
violation. Mattis v. Vaughn, 128 F.Supp.2d at 261. In other 
words, Rule 39 would be an attempt by the Tennessee 
Supreme Court to rewrite history by directing the federal 
courts to treat the Tennessee system as never having required 
Tenn. R. App. P. 11 applications to the Tennessee Supreme 
Court for discretionary review. See Wenger v. Frank, 266 


® Furthermore, Tenn. S. Ct. R. 39 raises a serious separation of 
powers question under the Tennessee Constitution. All legislative 
authority is vested in the General Assembly. Tenn. Const. art. II, § 3. The 
courts are expressly forbidden by the constitution from exercising any of 
the powers belonging to the legislative department. Tenn. Const. art. Il, § 
2; Ballentine v. Mayor of Pulaski, 83 Tenn. 633 (1885). 


275 


F.3d 218, 226 (3rd Cir. 2001).’ 


. Respondent submits that the only constitutional 
interpretation of Tenn. S. Ct. R. 39 is one reading the Rule as 
a statement that, effective immediately, the ordinary appeals 
process in Tennessee no longer necessarily includes Tenn. R. 
App. P. 11 applications. 


Vv 


OTHER VALID, PRACTICAL 
CONSIDERATIONS 


Even if the Supremacy Clause problem did not exist, there 
are other, valid reasons why Tenn. S. Ct. R. 39 should not be 
applied retrospectively. 

First, the purpose of Rule 39 is to discourage petitions for 
discretionary review under Tenn. R. App. P. 11. “Permission 
to appeal will be granted. . . only where special and important 
reasons justify the exercise of that discretionary review 
power.” Tenn. S. Ct. R. 39. As such, Rule 39 is not designed 
to benefit state prisoners seeking to litigate claims that were 
previously procedurally defaulted in federal court. Rather, the 
obvious purpose is an attempt by the Tennessee Supreme 
Court to control its docket. Such a purpose is not served by 
retrospective application of Rule 39. Any docket relief for the 
Tennessee Supreme Court could be prospective only. See 
Wenger v. Frank, 266 F.3d at 225. 


Second, making Rule 39 retrospective would serve only to 
greatly increase the burden on federal courts without 
providing any additional relief to the Tennessee Supreme 
Court. It begs the obvious to note that, over the years, 
numerous habeas claims have been denied because they were 
procedurally defaulted due to a petitioner’s failure to raise 


” Indeed, the preamble to Tenn. S. Ct. 39 itself contains a tacit 
admission of the recognized state of the law on exhaustion of state 
remedies regarding the need to file a Tenn. R. App. P. 11 application 
wherein the court states that, "criminal and post conviction relief litigants 
routinely petitioned" for Rule 11 relief in order to exhaust all available 
state remedies for purposes of federal habeas corpus litigation." 


276 


them to the Tennessee Supreme Court. To accept petitioner’s 
position, every habeas corpus decision since | July 1967, 
wherein procedural default was enforced because a petitioner 
failed to exhaust state remedies through Rule !1 application 
to the Tennessee Supreme Court, would be subject to 
reopening even where, as here, judgment is final. 

Third, there can be no serious argument that petitioner has 
relied upon the newly-created provisions of Rule 39 to his 
detriment, although this would be a legitimate concern for the 
federal courts in regard to Rule 39’s prospective application. 
See O'Sullivan v. Boerckel, 562 U.S. at 862 (Stevens, J., 
dissenting) (federal courts should not find procedural default 
where state prisoner has relied to his detriment on state 
supreme court’s explicit statement that he need not present 
every claim to it in order to exhaust state remedies). 


VI 


PETITIONER’S RELIANCE ON FED. R. CIV. P 
60(b)(6) IS INAPPROPRIATE 


Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(b) provides six grounds under which a 
party may seek relief from a judgment or order. It appears 
from a plain reading of the Rule and petitioner’s motion that 
he seeks to travel under the sixth ground: “any other reason 
justifying relief from the operation of the judgment.” 

“Rule 60(b) should apply only in exceptional or 
extraordinary circumstances which are not addressed by the 
first five numbered clauses of the Rule.”’ Olle v. Henry & 
Wright Corporation, 910 F.2d 357, 365 (6th Cir. 1990), reh'g 
denied. It is invoked only in those “unusual and extreme 
situations where principles of equity mandate relief.” Jd. 
(emphasis in original). Rule 60 (b) (6) exists to allow courts 
to vacate judgments whenever such action is appropriate to 
accomplish justice in extraordinary circumstances. Klapprott 
v. United States, 335 U.S. 601, 614-15, 69 S.Ct. 384, 390, 93 
L.Ed. 266 (1949). Accordingly, respondent submits that even 
petitioner’s proffered reason, the retrospective application of 
Rule 39, does not meet this criteria. Petitioner could not have 


277 


relied on the substance of this rule to his detriment because, at 
the time of petitioner’s travel through the state court review 
process, the well-established law in this Circuit required 
exhaustion through the highest state court. 


Furthermore, Rule 60(b) incorporates the concept of lack 
of fault on the part of a petitioner. Here, where petitioner, 
knowing what the law of this circuit was regarding 
exhaustion, purposely sought Rule 11 relief as to some 
claims, but not others, he should not now be heard to 
complain. Burke v. United States, 1999 WL 1065217 
(E.D.Pa. 1999) (copy attached). Free, calculated, deliberate 
choices are not appropriate for Rule 60(b) (6) relief. 
Norgaard v. DePuy Orthopaedics, Inc., 121 F.3d 1074 
1076 (7th 1997). 


Vil. 
CONCLUSION 


Based upon | the foregoing, respondent respectfully 
requests that petitioner’s motion for relief from judgment 
pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(b) be transferred to the Sixth 


Circuit Court of Appeals as a second or successive petition, or 
that it be denied outright. 


Respectfully submitted, 
PAUL G. SUMMERS 
Attorney General & Reporter

IN THE SUPREME COURT OF TENNESSEE 
AT NASHVILLE 


IN RE: ORDER ESTABLISHING RULE 39, 
RULES OF THE SUPREME COURT OF TENNESSEE: 
EXHAUSTION OF REMEDIES [APPENDIX B TO 
PETITIONER’S MEMORANDUM IN SUPPORT OF 
MOTION FOR RELIEF FROM JUDGMENT PURSUANT 
TO FED. R. CIV. P. 60(B)]. 


In 1967, the General Assembly created the Tennessee 
Court of Criminal Appeals in order to reduce the appellate 
backlog in criminal cases. In most criminal and post- 
conviction cases, review of a final order of the Court of 
Criminal Appeals is not a matter of right, but of sound 
judicial discretion. Permission to appeal will be granted by 
this Court only where special and important reasons justify 
the exercise of that discretionary review power, Tenn. R 
App. Proc. 11. We recognize that criminal and post- 
conviction relief litigants have routinely petitioned this Court 
for permission to appeal upon the Court of Criminal 
Appeals’ denial of relief in order to exhaust all available 
state remedies for purposes of federal habeas corpus 
litigation. In order to clarify that denial of relief by the Court 
of Criminal Appeals shall constitute exhaustion of state 
remedies for federal habeas corpus purposes, we hereby 
adopt the following Rule 39, Rules of the Supreme Court, 
as stated. below. 

In all appeals from criminal convictions on post- 

conviction relief matters from and after July 1, 

1967, a litigant shall not be required to petition for 

rehearing or to file an application for permission to 

appeal to the Supreme Court of Tennessee 
following an adverse decision of the Court of 

Criminal Appeals in order to be deemed to have 

exhausted all available state remedies respecting a 


279 


claim of error. Rather, when the claim has been 
presented to the Court of Criminal Appeals or the 
Supreme Court, and relief has been denied, the 
litigant Shall be deemed to have exhausted all 
available state remedies available for that claim. On 
automatic review of capital cases by the Supreme 
Court pursuant to Tennessee Code Annotated, § 39- 
13-206, a claim presented to the Court of Criminal 
Appeals shall be considered exhausted even when 


such claim is not renewed in the Supreme Court on 
automatic review. 


FOR THE COURT: 
Riley Anderson, Chief Justice
